I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still showing up and doing the main things. But it’s been harder than usual. I have to double-check myself more, like I don’t quite trust that I didn’t miss something obvious.

Yeah… I have. I mean, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend, getting everything set up just right. Or fishing—though half the time it was more about being by the water than catching anything, you know?

But “anxious” makes me think of, like, someone pacing before a big presentation—which, actually, I used to hate presentations in school, even though half the time the teacher barely listened—but yeah… I don’t know. I haven’t really pinned it down as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately there’s just this heavy, low feeling, like I’m getting through work and then coming home and everything’s kind of… muted. Like the color’s turned down. I still do what I’m supposed to do, mostly, but it just feels flat. And then I’ll get distracted thinking about some random thing, like whether I should’ve picked a different line of work back when I finished high school, which is ridiculous because that was forever ago, but… yeah. Down is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I wanted to admit, honestly. Looking back, these low, guilty stretches have been kind of hanging around off and on for maybe eleven months. Not every single minute, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to look forward to little stuff, like grilling on the weekend or going fishing if the weather was decent. Or even just putting on a record and sitting with it for a while—my dad had this old turntable, actually, and I always liked the whole routine of it. But lately the grill stuff just sits there, the fishing gear’s in the same spot, records too… and I just kind of don’t care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt even worse than your usual low feeling, almost to the point of being unable to function?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still showing up and doing the main things. But it’s been harder than usual. I have to double-check myself more, like I don’t quite trust that I didn’t miss something obvious.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people. Friends, family… I just don’t trust my mood or even my judgment lately, so it feels easier to keep to myself. I guess that’s not “unable to function,” but it’s definitely not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend, getting everything set up just right. Or fishing—though half the time it was more about being by the water than catching anything, you know?\n\nAnd records too. I’ve got this little stack I always meant to clean and sort, and normally I’d enjoy that whole process. Lately, though, I just look at the grill or the fishing gear or the records sitting there and… I don’t really care enough to do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get into something once I start, like grilling on Saturday or going fishing if it’s nice out, but then the stuff just stays where it is. The grill cover’s been on forever, my tackle box is just sitting there by the garage door.\n\nEven records—I used to like picking one out, cleaning it, the whole little ritual. My dad had me doing that when I was younger, actually, he was real particular about fingerprints. But lately I look at all of it and it’s like… I don’t really care enough to bother.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, a little. Not like I’ve sat down and made some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more… sometimes I’ll be lying there and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up in the morning.\n\nThat sounds worse when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I remember my uncle used to say sleep was the one time everybody gets a break, and lately that kind of sticks in my head more than it probably should. But no, I haven’t planned anything. It’s just those thoughts, kind of passing through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Not like I’m staying in bed all day, no. I’m still getting up and making it to work. But it’s… slower, I guess. I’ll sit in the car a few extra minutes before going in, or I’ll reread something three times because I don’t trust that I caught it right the first time.

I have to double-check myself more, like I don’t quite trust that I didn’t miss something obvious.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people. Friends, family… I just don’t trust my mood or even my judgment lately, so it feels easier to keep to myself. I guess that’s not “unable to function,” but it’s definitely not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend, getting everything set up just right. Or fishing—though half the time it was more about being by the water than catching anything, you know?\n\nAnd records too. I’ve got this little stack I always meant to clean and sort, and normally I’d enjoy that whole process. Lately, though, I just look at the grill or the fishing gear or the records sitting there and… I don’t really care enough to do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get into something once I start, like grilling on Saturday or going fishing if it’s nice out, but then the stuff just stays where it is. The grill cover’s been on forever, my tackle box is just sitting there by the garage door.\n\nEven records—I used to like picking one out, cleaning it, the whole little ritual. My dad had me doing that when I was younger, actually, he was real particular about fingerprints. But lately I look at all of it and it’s like… I don’t really care enough to bother.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, a little. Not like I’ve sat down and made some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more… sometimes I’ll be lying there and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up in the morning.\n\nThat sounds worse when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I remember my uncle used to say sleep was the one time everybody gets a break, and lately that kind of sticks in my head more than it probably should. But no, I haven’t planned anything. It’s just those thoughts, kind of passing through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the main things I usually look forward to—grilling on the weekend, going fishing, putting on a record—lately I just kind of leave all the stuff sitting there.\n\nLike the grill’s right there, and normally I’d be fussing with charcoal or whatever, but now I just walk past it. Same with the fishing gear. I don’t know if it’s “almost all,” exactly, but the things that used to feel like mine don’t really give me much of anything right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where these low feelings were so intense that you couldn't get out of bed or perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m staying in bed all day, no. I’m still getting up and making it to work. But it’s… slower, I guess. I’ll sit in the car a few extra minutes before going in, or I’ll reread something three times because I don’t trust that I caught it right the first time.\n\nI work with systems, so normally checking things is part of it anyway, you know, but lately it feels different—like I’m double-checking myself because I don’t trust my mood or my judgment. And I’ve kind of pulled back from people too. Friends, family. I just don’t want to say the wrong thing or be off around them, so I keep to myself more.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much right now. I keep thinking maybe I’ll feel better if I just do something small, like put on a record while I’m making coffee or mess with the grill a little on the weekend, but then I just… don’t.\n\nThe fishing stuff is sitting there too. I used to get kind of excited getting it all ready, checking the line and all that—my dad had this old tackle box that smelled like lake water and metal, funny what you remember—but lately I look at it and it just feels like another thing. So, no, I don’t really have much I’m looking forward to, even the little stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I’m not great with dates, honestly—I always think I’ll remember stuff because I work with systems and timelines all day, but my own life turns into a blur.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low, guilty stretches have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not every single day exactly the same, but I can’t say there’s been any real stretch where it felt like it lifted and stayed lifted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, normally I’d say the weekend stuff. But lately the grill stuff just sits there, the fishing gear’s in the same spot, records too… and I just kind of don’t care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt even worse than your usual low feeling, almost to the point of being unable to function?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped functioning, exactly. I have to double-check myself more, like I don’t quite trust that I didn’t miss something obvious.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people. Friends, family… I just don’t trust my mood or even my judgment lately, so it feels easier to keep to myself. I guess that’s not “unable to function,” but it’s definitely not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend, getting everything set up just right. I’ve got this little stack I always meant to clean and sort, and normally I’d enjoy that whole process. Lately, though, I just look at the grill or the fishing gear or the records sitting there and… I don’t really care enough to do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get into something once I start, like grilling on Saturday or going fishing if it’s nice out, but then the stuff just stays where it is. My dad had me doing that when I was younger, actually, he was real particular about fingerprints. I’m still getting up and making it to work. But it’s… slower, I guess. I’ll sit in the car a few extra minutes before going in, or I’ll reread something three times because I don’t trust that I caught it right the first time.\n\nI work with systems, so normally checking things is part of it anyway, you know, but lately it feels different—like I’m double-checking myself because I don’t trust my mood or my judgment. And then I’ll get distracted thinking about some random thing, like whether I should’ve picked a different line of work back when I finished high school, which is ridiculous because that was forever ago, but… yeah. Down is probably the word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I wanted to admit, honestly. Looking back, these low, guilty stretches have been kind of hanging around off and on for maybe eleven months. Not every single minute, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to look forward to little stuff, like grilling on the weekend or going fishing if the weather was decent. Or even just putting on a record and sitting with it for a while—my dad had this old turntable, actually, and I always liked the whole routine of it. But lately the grill stuff just sits there, the fishing gear’s in the same spot, records too… and I just kind of don’t care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt even worse than your usual low feeling, almost to the point of being unable to function?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still showing up and doing the main things. But it’s been harder than usual. I have to double-check myself more, like I don’t quite trust that I didn’t miss something obvious.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people. Friends, family… I just don’t trust my mood or even my judgment lately, so it feels easier to keep to myself. I guess that’s not “unable to function,” but it’s definitely not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend, getting everything set up just right. Or fishing—though half the time it was more about being by the water than catching anything, you know?\n\nAnd records too. I’ve got this little stack I always meant to clean and sort, and normally I’d enjoy that whole process. Lately, though, I just look at the grill or the fishing gear or the records sitting there and… I don’t really care enough to do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get into something once I start, like grilling on Saturday or going fishing if it’s nice out, but then the stuff just stays where it is. The grill cover’s been on forever, my tackle box is just sitting there by the garage door.\n\nEven records—I used to like picking one out, cleaning it, the whole little ritual. My dad had me doing that when I was younger, actually, he was real particular about fingerprints. But lately I look at all of it and it’s like… I don’t really care enough to bother.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, a little. Not like I’ve sat down and made some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more… sometimes I’ll be lying there and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up in the morning.\n\nThat sounds worse when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I remember my uncle used to say sleep was the one time everybody gets a break, and lately that kind of sticks in my head more than it probably should. But no, I haven’t planned anything. It’s just those thoughts, kind of passing through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the main things I usually look forward to—grilling on the weekend, going fishing, putting on a record—lately I just kind of leave all the stuff sitting there.\n\nLike the grill’s right there, and normally I’d be fussing with charcoal or whatever, but now I just walk past it. Same with the fishing gear. I don’t know if it’s “almost all,” exactly, but the things that used to feel like mine don’t really give me much of anything right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where these low feelings were so intense that you couldn't get out of bed or perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m staying in bed all day, no. I’m still getting up and making it to work. But it’s… slower, I guess. I’ll sit in the car a few extra minutes before going in, or I’ll reread something three times because I don’t trust that I caught it right the first time.\n\nI work with systems, so normally checking things is part of it anyway, you know, but lately it feels different—like I’m double-checking myself because I don’t trust my mood or my judgment. And I’ve kind of pulled back from people too. Friends, family. I just don’t want to say the wrong thing or be off around them, so I keep to myself more.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much right now. I keep thinking maybe I’ll feel better if I just do something small, like put on a record while I’m making coffee or mess with the grill a little on the weekend, but then I just… don’t.\n\nThe fishing stuff is sitting there too. I used to get kind of excited getting it all ready, checking the line and all that—my dad had this old tackle box that smelled like lake water and metal, funny what you remember—but lately I look at it and it just feels like another thing. So, no, I don’t really have much I’m looking forward to, even the little stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I’m not great with dates, honestly—I always think I’ll remember stuff because I work with systems and timelines all day, but my own life turns into a blur.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low, guilty stretches have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not every single day exactly the same, but I can’t say there’s been any real stretch where it felt like it lifted and stayed lifted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, normally I’d say the weekend stuff. Grilling, especially—nothing fancy, just standing out there messing with the coals or the temperature, maybe putting a record on inside while I’m getting things ready. And fishing used to be something I’d sort of look forward to even before I went, like checking the gear, making sure I had everything.\n\nBut lately I just leave it all sitting there. The grill cover stays on, the records stay on the shelf, fishing stuff just kind of sits in the corner. I look at it and think, “I should do that,” but I don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from activities you used to enjoy without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… looking back, it’s been around eleven months or so. Not in one neat block, exactly—more like these low, guilty stretches that keep coming back and hanging around.\n\nI remember thinking at first it was just a bad week or I was tired from work, because everything sort of runs together when you’re staring at screens all day, you know? But it hasn’t really cleared in any lasting way. So… about eleven months, off and on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but not much. Like, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend—getting the charcoal right, messing with the timing, all that. Or fishing, even just getting the gear together. And records too, I’ve got a few I always used to put on when I wanted to unwind.\n\nLately, though, the grill just sits there with the cover on, fishing stuff’s still in the corner, records stay on the shelf. I’ll think, “Yeah, I should do that,” and then… I just don’t really care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy most of the time over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think so. I mean, I keep circling around the same stuff—like weekends, I used to be thinking about grilling by Thursday, you know, what to cook, whether the charcoal was still good. Or I’d put on a record while I messed around the house.\n\nAnd fishing too, though that’s a whole production sometimes, because if you forget one little thing it ruins the morning. But lately all of it just kind of sits there. The grill’s covered, the records stay on the shelf, the fishing stuff’s in the corner, and I’ll look at it like I should want to do it… but I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekend—getting the charcoal right, messing with the timing, all that. Or fishing, even just getting the gear together. And records too, I’ve got a few I always used to put on when I wanted to unwind.\n\nLately, though, the grill just sits there with the cover on, fishing stuff’s still in the corner, records stay on the shelf. I’ll think, “Yeah, I should do that,” and then… I just don’t really care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy most of the time over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think so. I mean, I keep circling around the same stuff—like weekends, I used to be thinking about grilling by Thursday, you know, what to cook, whether the charcoal was still good. Or I’d put on a record while I messed around the house.\n\nAnd fishing too, though that’s a whole production sometimes, because if you forget one little thing it ruins the morning. But lately all of it just kind of sits there. The grill’s covered, the records stay on the shelf, the fishing stuff’s in the corner, and I’ll look at it like I should want to do it… but I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in things you used to enjoy for most of the past two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I usually look forward to grilling on the weekend, fishing, or putting on a record, but lately I just leave the stuff sitting there and don’t really care.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, it’s not like I made a list or anything, but most days lately I’ll look at the grill or the fishing gear and just… leave it there. I used to look forward to that stuff, especially on weekends. Even putting on a record feels like more effort than it should.

Funny thing is, I still keep thinking I should clean the grill because the cover gets all dusty, and then I remember I bought this brush for it that was supposed to be better, but… yeah, I don’t actually do any of it. I just don’t really care enough to start.

I mean, the main things I usually look forward to—grilling on the weekend, going fishing, putting on a record—lately I just kind of leave all the stuff sitting there.\n\nLike the grill’s right there, and normally I’d be fussing with charcoal or whatever, but now I just walk past it. Same with the fishing gear. I don’t know if it’s “almost all,” exactly, but the things that used to feel like mine don’t really give me much of anything right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where these low feelings were so intense that you couldn't get out of bed or perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m staying in bed all day, no. I’m still getting up and making it to work. But it’s… slower, I guess. I’ll sit in the car a few extra minutes before going in, or I’ll reread something three times because I don’t trust that I caught it right the first time.\n\nI work with systems, so normally checking things is part of it anyway, you know, but lately it feels different—like I’m double-checking myself because I don’t trust my mood or my judgment. And I’ve kind of pulled back from people too. Friends, family. I just don’t want to say the wrong thing or be off around them, so I keep to myself more.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, no matter how small they might seem?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not much right now. So, no, I don’t really have much I’m looking forward to, even the little stuff.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I’m not great with dates, honestly—I always think I’ll remember stuff because I work with systems and timelines all day, but my own life turns into a blur.\n\nLooking back, I’d say these low, guilty stretches have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not every single day exactly the same, but I can’t say there’s been any real stretch where it felt like it lifted and stayed lifted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, normally I’d say the weekend stuff.
